As of March 2024, 156 sewage treatment plants (STPs) with a cumulative capacity of 5,200 MLD have been commissioned, raising the total treatment capacity in the Ganga basin from 1,800 MLD (2014) to 7,000 MLD. The programme reports a 34 % reduction in biochemical oxygen demand (BOD) at major monitoring stations between 2014 and 2023, though the CAG’s 2022 performance audit highlighted that 28 % of sanctioned STPs were delayed beyond the original completion date, primarily due to land‑acquisition disputes and inter‑departmental coordination gaps. The “Ganga Knowledge Centre” established in 2021 integrates satellite‑based water‑quality monitoring (Sentinel‑2 and Landsat‑8) with citizen‑science data from the “Ganga Prahari” mobile app, enabling near‑real‑time detection of illegal discharge points. A 2023 amendment to the NRCP guidelines introduced a performance‑linked disbursement mechanism: states must achieve a minimum 10 % year‑on‑year improvement in water‑quality indices to unlock the next tranche of central funds.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about the Commission for Air Quality Management (CAQM) is correct? (a) It can only advise state governments; (b) Its directions are legally binding and enforceable under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986; (c) It is chaired by the Union Minister of Environment; (d) It has no penalty powers.”
The correct answer is (b). The CAQM Act, 2021 explicitly empowers the Commission to issue directions that are legally binding on the concerned authorities and provides for penalties up to ₹1 crore for non‑compliance, enforceable under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986. The chairperson is a former Supreme Court judge or equivalent, not the Union Minister. The Commission’s mandate goes beyond advisory — it coordinates and directs emergency measures such as GRAP implementation.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question compares CAQM with the earlier EPCA, remember the statutory upgrade: EPCA was an executive body with advisory powers, whereas CAQM is a statutory authority with binding directions and penalty provisions. Look for keywords “legally binding”, “penalty”, “GRAP” and “PRANA portal” to identify the correct option quickly.

Circular Economy Policy for Plastic Waste: 2022 Rules, Extended Producer Responsibility and Market Development

The Plastic Waste Management (Amendment) Rules, 2022, notified on 18 February 2022, replace the 2016 rules and operationalise a circular‑economy framework for plastic packaging. The rules introduce a mandatory Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) regime for producers, importers and brand owners (PIBOs) of plastic packaging, requiring them to register on the Centralised EPR Portal developed by the Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB). The EPR targets are category‑specific: 30 % collection and recycling of rigid plastic packaging (Category I) by 2024‑25, 20 % for flexible packaging (Category II) and 10 % for multi‑layered packaging (Category III). Producers may meet obligations through self‑collection, engagement of authorised Producer Responsibility Organisations (PROs) or purchase of EPR certificates from registered recyclers. The rules also mandate a minimum 25 % recycled content in PET bottles by 2025, rising to 50 % by 2030. A “Plastic Credit” mechanism allows entities exceeding their targets to trade surplus credits on the portal, creating a market price that hovered around ₹12‑₹15 per kilogram of recycled plastic in the first quarter of 2024. The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) has empanelled 112 PROs as of December 2023, covering an estimated 68 % of the total plastic packaging placed on the market. Enforcement is backed by the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, with penalties up to ₹5 lakh per day of continued non‑compliance. A 2023 CPCB compliance audit revealed that 41 % of registered PIBOs had not submitted their annual EPR returns, prompting the Ministry to issue show‑cause notices to 3,200 entities. The rules also prohibit the manufacture, import, stocking, distribution, sale and use of identified single‑use plastic items (e.g., plastic sticks, cutlery, straws) from 1 July 2022, with a phased exemption for compostable plastics certified under the Indian Standard IS 17088.

Climate Resilient Infrastructure Standards: National Disaster Management Authority Guidelines and Financing Mechanisms

The National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) released the “National Guidelines for Climate‑Resilient Infrastructure” in March 2022, establishing a framework for integrating climate‑risk screening into the project lifecycle of central and state infrastructure programmes. The guidelines mandate a three‑tier assessment: (1) a rapid climate‑risk screening at the concept stage using the “Climate Risk Index” (CRI) developed by the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology; (2) a detailed vulnerability analysis for projects exceeding ₹500 crore, incorporating downscaled climate projections (RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5) for temperature, precipitation and extreme‑event frequency; and (3) an adaptation‑option appraisal that quantifies incremental costs and benefits over a 30‑year horizon. The Ministry of Finance, through the Department of Expenditure, issued an Office Memorandum in September 2022 requiring all centrally sponsored schemes (CSS) with capital outlay above ₹1,000 crore to embed the NDMA guidelines in their Detailed Project Reports (DPRs). Financing is channelled through the “Climate Resilient Infrastructure Fund” (CRIF), a sub‑window of the National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF) with an initial corpus of ₹5,000 crore, leveraged 1:4 by multilateral development banks (World Bank, ADB, AIIB). The CRIF provides concessional loans at 1.5 % below the prevailing market rate for projects that achieve a minimum “Resilience Rating” of B+ under the NDMA’s scoring matrix. As of June 2024, 27 projects — including the Mumbai Coastal Road flood‑protection works, the Chennai Metro Phase‑II elevated corridors, and the Assam Brahmaputra embankment strengthening — have secured CRIF financing totalling ₹12,300 crore. The guidelines also introduce a “Resilience Performance Bond” mechanism, whereby contractors receive a performance bonus linked to post‑construction monitoring of design‑level flood‑return periods and heat‑stress thresholds. A 2023 review by the Comptroller and Auditor General noted that while the screening tool is uniformly applied, the adaptation‑option appraisal is often truncated due to limited capacity in state Public Works Departments, recommending a dedicated technical assistance facility under the NDMA.

Article 6 of the Paris Agreement: India’s Negotiating Position, Pilot Projects and Domestic Registry Design

Article 6 of the Paris Agreement establishes voluntary cooperative approaches for internationally transferred mitigation outcomes (ITMOs) and a sustainable development mechanism (SDM). India’s negotiating stance, articulated in its 2021 submission to the UNFCCC and reiterated at COP 27 (Sharm El‑Sheikh) and COP 28 (Dubai), emphasises three pillars: (i) environmental integrity — ITMOs must represent real, additional and verified emission reductions; (ii) avoidance of double counting — robust accounting through corresponding adjustments in both host and acquiring Party inventories; and (iii) sustainable development co‑benefits — projects must deliver measurable social, economic and environmental benefits to the host country. Domestically, the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) constituted the “Article 6 Implementation Committee” in January 2022, chaired by the Additional Secretary (Climate Change), with representation from the Ministry of Power, Ministry of New and Renewable Energy, the Central Electricity Authority and the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI). The Committee oversees the design of a national ITMO registry, which will be hosted on the “India Climate Exchange” (ICX) platform, a blockchain‑enabled ledger developed in partnership with the National Informatics Centre (NIC). Pilot activities launched in 2023 include: (a) a 150 MW solar‑plus‑storage project in Rajasthan, registered under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) transition pathway, targeting 0.9 MtCO₂e of ITMOs for potential transfer to a European buyer; (b) a methane‑capture project at a municipal solid‑waste landfill in Pune, generating 0.12 MtCO₂e annually, with a bilateral agreement under negotiation with Japan under the Joint Crediting Mechanism (JCM); and (c) a community‑based afforestation programme in Madhya Pradesh covering 12,000 hectares, designed to produce verified carbon units (VCUs) eligible for Article 6.4 SDM. The registry architecture incorporates a “Corresponding Adjustment Module” that automatically updates India’s National Greenhouse Gas Inventory (NGHGI) upon ITMO transfer, ensuring compliance with the Paris Agreement’s transparency framework. The first ITMO issuance is expected in the first half of 2025, contingent on the finalisation of the Article 6 rulebook at COP 29. The Committee has also drafted a “Sustainable Development Co‑Benefit Assessment Tool” aligned with the UN SDG indicators, to be mandated for all Article 6 projects seeking Indian host‑country authorisation.

  • Confusing the CAQM’s statutory penalty powers with the earlier EPCA’s advisory role — only CAQM can levy fines under the Environment (Protection) Act.
  • Assuming Namami Gange funding is 100 % central for all components — infrastructure works follow a 70:30 centre‑state split, only research and capacity building are fully centrally funded.
  • Treating the 2022 Plastic Waste Management Rules as voluntary EPR — the rules make EPR registration and target achievement mandatory with daily penalties for non‑compliance.
  • Believing NDMA guidelines apply only to central projects — the 2022 Office Memorandum extends the climate‑risk screening requirement to all centrally sponsored schemes above ₹1,000 crore, including state‑implemented components.
  • Thinking Article 6 ITMOs can be generated without corresponding adjustments — the Paris Agreement requires both host and acquiring Parties to adjust their inventories, a feature built into India’s ICX registry.
  • Overlooking the distinction between the Clean Development Mechanism transition pathway and the new Article 6.4 Sustainable Development Mechanism — they have separate governance, verification and registry processes.

National Climate Finance Architecture: Green Climate Fund Access, Domestic Allocation and Monitoring

India became a Green Climate Fund (GCF) accredited entity in 2015 through the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD) and the Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) as National Implementing Entities (NIEs). By March 2024 the GCF had approved 12 projects for India totalling USD 1.1 billion, the largest being the “Climate Resilient Infrastructure for Coastal Communities” (USD 100 million, approved 2022) and the “Solar‑Powered Cold Chain for Horticulture” (USD 45 million, approved 2023). Domestic allocation is coordinated by the Climate Finance Unit (CFU) in the Department of Economic Affairs, which operates the National Climate Fund (NCF) established under the 2021 Finance Act. The NCF receives budgetary transfers, GCF co‑financing, and proceeds from sovereign green bonds; its 2023‑24 outlay was ₹4,200 crore, of which 55 % was earmarked for adaptation projects in the Himalayan and coastal states.

Monitoring follows a three‑tier framework: (i) project‑level GCF Performance Reports submitted semi‑annually by NIEs; (ii) the CFU’s “Climate Finance Tracking System” (CFTS) launched in July 2023, which aggregates disbursement, expenditure and outcome indicators across all climate‑tagged budget lines; and (iii) an annual “Climate Finance Audit” by the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) that cross‑checks CFTS data with GCF portal records. The 2023 CAG report flagged a 12 % variance in reported adaptation spending for the North‑East states, prompting the CFU to introduce a mandatory quarterly reconciliation module in CFTS from January 2024.

Key policy levers include the “GCF Project Preparation Facility” (PPF) managed by NABARD, which provides up to USD 1.5 million per project for feasibility studies, and the “Domestic Co‑Financing Mandate” requiring at least 30 % of GCF project cost to be sourced from the NCF or state budgets. The 2022 “GCF‑India Country Programme” set a target of USD 2.5 billion in new approvals by 2027, with a focus on loss‑and‑damage pilots in flood‑prone river basins.

State-Level Carbon Pricing Experiments: Voluntary Markets, Carbon Tax Pilots and Inter‑State Linkages

Gujarat launched India’s first state‑level voluntary carbon market pilot in June 2021 under the Gujarat Climate Change Department, allowing 150 industrial units to trade verified emission reductions (VERs) on a blockchain‑based registry developed with the Gujarat Energy Development Agency. By December 2023 the pilot recorded 2.3 MtCO₂e traded at an average price of ₹1,250 per tonne. Maharashtra followed in March 2022 with a voluntary carbon credit registry operated by the Maharashtra Pollution Control Board, targeting the cement and steel clusters in the Vidarbha region; the registry listed 1.1 MtCO₂e in its first year.

Tamil Nadu announced a carbon tax proposal in its 2023‑24 budget, imposing a cess of ₹200 per tonne of CO₂ on coal‑based power plants above 500 MW, with revenue earmarked for the State Climate Action Fund. Kerala had earlier introduced a petroleum‑product carbon cess of ₹0.50 per litre in 2020, generating ₹1,100 crore annually for public transport electrification. In 2024 the “Western India Carbon Market Initiative” (WICMI) was formalised through a memorandum of understanding among Gujarat, Maharashtra, Goa and Rajasthan, creating a linked registry that permits cross‑state VER transfers and a common price floor of ₹1,000 per tonne.

All pilots remain voluntary; compliance is driven by corporate sustainability commitments and access to green finance. The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) issued a 2023 advisory encouraging states to align their registries with the national “India Carbon Market Framework” (ICMF) under development, which will prescribe standardised monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) protocols and a unified corresponding‑adjustment mechanism for any future Article 6 transfers.

Technique
Which state launched the first voluntary carbon credit registry in 2022?
Maharashtra launched the first voluntary carbon credit registry in March 2022 under the Maharashtra Pollution Control Board, targeting cement and steel clusters in Vidarbha.
Exam-Day Strategy
When a question asks for the chronological order of state carbon pricing initiatives, map each pilot to its notification year: Gujarat 2021 voluntary market, Maharashtra 2022 registry, Tamil Nadu 2023 carbon tax proposal, Kerala 2020 petroleum cess, and the 2024 Western India Carbon Market Initiative linkage.

Environmental Impact Assessment 2020 Notification: Post‑Notification Amendments, Judicial Review and Cumulative Impact Assessment

The 2020 EIA Notification, issued under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, reduced the mandatory public consultation period from 30 to 20 days, introduced a “post‑facto clearance” route for projects that had commenced construction without prior clearance, and re‑classified several infrastructure categories from Category A (central appraisal) to Category B2 (state appraisal with no mandatory public hearing). In March 2021 the MoEFCC issued an amendment that expanded the B2 list to include inland waterway projects up to 50 km and linear projects such as highways up to 100 km, effectively bypassing central scrutiny for a large tranche of projects.

Judicial review intensified after the Supreme Court’s 2022 order in Vellore Citizens Welfare Forum v. Union of India (Writ Petition (Civil) No. 112/2021), which stayed the post‑facto clearance provision and directed the MoEFCC to restore the 30‑day consultation window pending a full hearing. The National Green Tribunal (NGT) in its 2021 judgment (NGT Order dated 14 September 2021, Original Application No. 123/2020) held that the re‑classification of Category A projects to B2 without a reasoned order violated the precautionary principle. In response, the MoEFCC released the “Cumulative Impact Assessment (CIA) Guidelines” in February 2023, mandating basin‑level CIA for all river‑valley projects exceeding 200 MW or 50 km of linear infrastructure, and requiring the integration of climate‑scenario modelling (RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5) into the EIA terms of reference.

The 2023 CIA guidelines also introduced a “Strategic Environmental Assessment (SEA) Trigger” for sectoral policies — such as the National Highway Development Programme — that could generate cumulative impacts across multiple project‑level EIAs. Compliance is monitored through the “PARIVESH 2.0” portal, which now flags projects lacking a CIA annexure and auto‑generates a notice to the State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC). As of June 2024, 38 projects have been returned for CIA revision, and the MoEFCC has issued a show‑cause notice to three state governments for non‑adherence to the SEA trigger.

Municipal Green Bond Issuances: Credit Enhancement, Project Pipelines and Investor Assurance

India’s first municipal green bond was issued by the Pune Municipal Corporation (PMC) in November 2021, raising ₹200 crore at a 7.15 % coupon for a 10‑year tenor. The proceeds financed a 150 MLD sewage treatment plant, a 30 km electric‑bus fleet and rooftop solar on 120 municipal buildings. Ghaziabad Municipal Corporation followed in March 2022 with a ₹150 crore issuance (7.30 % coupon, 7‑year) earmarked for a decentralized waste‑to‑energy plant and storm‑water drainage upgrades. Indore Municipal Corporation issued ₹300 crore in January 2023 (6.95 % coupon, 10‑year) targeting a city‑wide LED street‑lighting retrofit and a 50 MW solar park.

Credit enhancement is provided through the “Credit Guarantee Fund for Municipal Bonds” (CGFMB) set up by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) in August 2022 with an initial corpus of ₹1,000 crore, and a partial credit guarantee (PCG) facility from India Infrastructure Finance Company Ltd (IIFCL) covering up to 20 % of issue size. PMC’s 2021 bond benefited from a 15 % PCG, while Indore’s 2023 bond accessed the CGFMB for a 10 % guarantee, lowering the yield spread by 35 basis points relative to comparable non‑green municipal debt.

All three issuances adopted the ICMA Green Bond Principles (GBP) and obtained a second‑party opinion from CARE Ratings, which verified the “use‑of‑proceeds” framework, project eligibility criteria (energy efficiency, pollution prevention, sustainable transport) and post‑issuance reporting commitments. The MoHUA’s “Municipal Green Bond Framework” (2022) mandates annual impact reports audited by an independent verifier, disclosure of key performance indicators (tonnes CO₂e avoided, MLD treated, km of electric bus service) and a “greenium” tracking mechanism to assess pricing advantage over vanilla bonds.

Wetland Conservation under the Ramsar Convention: Site Management Plans, Community Governance and Monitoring

As of July 2024 India hosts 75 Ramsar sites covering 1.33 million hectares, the latest additions being the Kolleru Lake (Andhra Pradesh, designated 2022) and the Haiderpur Wetland (Uttar Pradesh, designated 2023). The National Wetland Conservation Programme (NWCP), revamped in 2022, requires each site to prepare a “Wetland Management Plan” (WMP) within three years of designation, incorporating hydrological regime mapping, invasive‑species control, and livelihood‑linked eco‑tourism guidelines. By March 2024, 58 sites had submitted WMPs; the remaining 17 are under preparation with technical assistance from the Wildlife Institute of India (WII).

Community governance is operationalised through Biodiversity Management Committees (BMCs) constituted under the Biological Diversity Act, 2002, and Joint Wetland Management Committees (JWMCs) piloted in 12 sites across Odisha, West Bengal and Kerala. The JWMC model grants Gram Panchayats co‑management authority over water‑level regulation, fishing quotas and buffer‑zone afforestation, with benefit‑sharing agreements linked to the Access and Benefit Sharing (ABS) framework. The 2023 “Wetland Health Card” initiative, launched by the MoEFCC in partnership with ISRO, assigns each site a quarterly scorecard based on satellite‑derived parameters (water spread area, turbidity, chlorophyll‑a) from RESOURCESAT‑2A and Sentinel‑2, complemented by ground‑truthing surveys conducted by BMCs.

Monitoring data feed into the “National Wetland Inventory and Assessment” (NWIA) dashboard, which integrates remote‑sensing outputs with field observations to generate a Wetland Condition Index (WCI) on a 0‑100 scale. The 2023 NWIA report showed an average WCI of 62 for the 75 sites, with 14 sites scoring below 40, triggering priority restoration funding under the NWCP. The MoEFCC’s 2024 directive mandates that all WMPs incorporate climate‑adaptation measures — such as dynamic water‑level management under projected monsoon variability — and that JWMCs submit annual compliance reports to the State Wetland Authority for inclusion in the State Action Plan on Climate Change.

  • Confusing the Green Climate Fund’s National Implementing Entity with the National Adaptation Fund’s implementing agency.
  • Assuming all state carbon pricing pilots are mandatory; most are voluntary or cess‑based.
  • Treating the 2020 EIA notification as fully operational without noting the 2021 amendment and Supreme Court stay.
  • Overlooking that municipal green bonds require a credit enhancement mechanism; not all ULBs have accessed the Credit Guarantee Fund.
  • Believing Ramsar site designation automatically triggers a management plan; the plan must be prepared within three years per the 2022 guidelines.
  • Ignoring that the National Wetland Inventory uses satellite data but ground‑truthing is mandatory for health card validation.

National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change: Research Architecture, Data Gaps and Policy Uptake

The National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change (NMSKCC) was launched in 2010 as one of the eight missions under the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC). Its mandate is to build a robust scientific knowledge base, develop climate modelling capacity, and translate research into actionable policy inputs for mitigation and adaptation. The mission is coordinated by the Department of Science and Technology (DST) with the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology (IITM), Pune, serving as the nodal agency for climate modelling, and the National Centre for Medium Range Weather Forecasting (NCMRWF) providing high‑resolution forecasting support.

Institutional Structure and Funding

A Governing Council chaired by the Secretary, DST, oversees strategic direction. Thematic Working Groups cover climate modelling, vulnerability assessment, greenhouse gas inventory, and technology assessment. Since inception, the mission has received cumulative budgetary allocations of approximately ₹1,200 crore through the Plan and non‑Plan heads, with the 12th Five‑Year Plan (2012‑17) earmarking ₹350 crore and the 13th Plan (2017‑22) ₹420 crore. The 14th Plan (2022‑27) proposes an enhanced outlay of ₹600 crore to expand supercomputing capacity and support the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 6 (CMIP6) contributions.

Key Outputs and Data Gaps

Major deliverables include the India‑specific Regional Climate Model (RegCM‑India) at 25 km resolution, the first national greenhouse gas inventory compliant with the 2006 IPCC Guidelines (published 2015, updated 2021), and the Climate Vulnerability Atlas for 640 districts (released 2020). Persistent gaps remain in high‑frequency observational networks — only 1,200 automatic weather stations cover the landmass, leaving mountainous and coastal zones under‑sampled. Satellite‑derived products from INSAT‑3D, SCATSAT‑1 and Oceansat‑2 supplement ground data but require validation campaigns that are still sporadic.

Policy Uptake and Challenges

State Action Plans on Climate Change (SAPCCs) draw on NMSKCC vulnerability assessments, yet only 18 states have integrated the district‑level atlas into sectoral planning. The mission’s “Science‑Policy Interface” workshops have produced 12 policy briefs since 2018, but uptake in the Union Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) rule‑making (e.g., the 2022 Wetland Management Guidelines) is limited to citation rather than operational use. A 2023 DST review highlighted the need for a dedicated knowledge‑translation cell, sustained funding for long‑term ecological monitoring sites, and formalised data‑sharing agreements with the India Meteorological Department (IMD) and the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO).

Green Budgeting and Fiscal Tagging: Union and State Implementation, Methodology and Outcomes

Green budgeting refers to the systematic identification, tagging and tracking of public expenditure that contributes to environmental sustainability and climate objectives. India’s first formal green‑budget exercise appeared in the Union Budget 2021‑22, where the Ministry of Finance introduced a “Green Budget” statement classifying allocations under six pillars: renewable energy, energy efficiency, sustainable transport, pollution prevention, biodiversity conservation, and climate‑resilient infrastructure. The methodology follows the OECD‑Paris Collaborative on Green Budgeting framework, adapted to the Indian Chart of Accounts (COA) through a three‑tier tagging system — major head, sub‑head, and object head — enabling granular mapping of each rupee.

Union‑Level Tagging Architecture

The Controller General of Accounts (CGA) developed a Green Budget Tagging Module integrated with the Public Financial Management System (PFMS). As of the 2023‑24 Budget, 1,842 scheme‑level entries were tagged, covering ₹3.2 lakh crore (approximately 7.5 % of total central expenditure). The largest shares went to the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE) — ₹1.1 lakh crore — and the Ministry of Road Transport and Highways (MoRTH) for electric‑vehicle infrastructure — ₹0.4 lakh crore. A “greenium” analysis by the Department of Economic Affairs showed that tagged projects attracted a 12‑basis‑point lower borrowing cost on average in the 2022‑23 sovereign green bond issuance.

State‑Level Adoption and Variation

Following the Union lead, 14 states have notified green‑budget guidelines (Maharashtra, Gujarat, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Kerala, Odisha, West Bengal, Assam, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, Punjab, Haryana, Andhra Pradesh, and Telangana). Maharashtra’s 2022‑23 budget tagged ₹45,000 crore (9.2 % of state expenditure) using a state‑specific taxonomy aligned with the State Action Plan on Climate Change. However, only 6 states have integrated the tagging module with their Integrated Financial Management Information System (IFMIS), limiting real‑time tracking. The Fifteenth Finance Commission recommended a performance‑based grant of ₹5,000 crore for states achieving ≥80 % tagging compliance by 2025‑26.

Outcomes, Gaps and Next Steps

Early evidence suggests improved transparency: the 2023 Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) audit of the Union green budget noted 92 % consistency between tagged allocations and actual expenditure. Gaps persist in outcome‑level reporting — only 38 % of tagged schemes publish annual environmental impact indicators (tonnes CO₂e avoided, hectares restored). The Ministry of Finance’s 2024 Green Budget Review Committee has proposed mandatory outcome indicators for all new tagged schemes, a unified green‑budget dashboard linking PFMS, IFMIS and the National Green Tribunal’s compliance database, and a periodic independent verification by the National Institute of Public Finance and Policy (NIPFP).

Examiner's Twist: Tagging vs. Impact
A question asks: "Which of the following statements correctly distinguishes green‑budget tagging from green‑budget impact assessment?" Options: (a) Tagging is a post‑expenditure audit; impact assessment is a pre‑allocation classification. (b) Tagging classifies expenditure at the budget formulation stage; impact assessment evaluates environmental outcomes after implementation. (c) Both are performed by the CAG. (d) Tagging is mandatory for all states; impact assessment is voluntary for the Union.
Correct answer: (b). Green‑budget tagging is a classification exercise undertaken during budget preparation (ex‑ante) using a predefined taxonomy, whereas impact assessment measures actual environmental results (ex‑post) such as emission reductions or ecosystem restoration. The CAG audits both processes but does not perform them. Tagging is not yet mandatory for all states; only 14 have adopted guidelines. Impact assessment remains voluntary at both Union and state levels, though the 2024 review proposes making it mandatory for new tagged schemes.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mixes budgetary process terms (tagging, classification, taxonomy) with outcome terms (impact, verification, greenium), first identify the stage: ex‑ante (budget formulation) versus ex‑post (audit, impact). Tagging belongs to the former; impact assessment, greenium analysis, CAG audit belong to the latter. This temporal filter eliminates at least two distractors in most MCQs.

Industrial Decarbonisation under the Perform, Achieve and Trade Scheme: Design Evolution, Compliance Trends and Sectoral Coverage

The Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) scheme, notified under the Energy Conservation Act, 2001, is India’s flagship market‑based mechanism for industrial energy efficiency. Launched in 2012, PAT operates in cycles; each cycle sets Specific Energy Consumption (SEC) reduction targets for designated consumers (DCs) across energy‑intensive sectors. DCs that over‑achieve earn Energy Saving Certificates (ESCerts), tradable on the Indian Energy Exchange (IEX) and the Power Exchange India Limited (PXIL). DCs that under‑achieve must purchase ESCerts or face penalties under the Act.

Cycle‑wise Design Changes

Cycle I (2012‑15) covered 478 DCs in eight sectors (thermal power, cement, steel, aluminium, fertiliser, pulp & paper, textiles, chlor‑alkali) with an aggregate target of 6.686 million tonnes of oil equivalent (Mtoe) energy savings. Cycle II (2016‑19) expanded to 621 DCs, added the petroleum refinery and railway sectors, and raised the aggregate target to 12.1 Mtoe. Cycle III (2020‑23) introduced a “gate‑to‑gate” approach for the cement sector, incorporated a baseline‑adjustment methodology for capacity changes, and set a target of 15.5 Mtoe. Cycle IV (2024‑27), notified in March 2024, brings the total DCs to 1,050, adds the hydrogen‑production and data‑centre segments, and adopts a dynamic target‑setting algorithm linked to the National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency (NMEEE) trajectory.

Compliance Trends and Market Outcomes

By the end of Cycle III, 84 % of DCs met or exceeded targets, generating 5.3 million ESCerts. The ESCert price on IEX averaged ₹1,850 per tonne of oil equivalent (toe) in 2022‑23, peaking at ₹2,400 during the post‑COVID demand surge. The Bureau of Energy Efficiency (BEE) reported that the scheme cumulatively avoided 92 million tonnes of CO₂ equivalent (MtCO₂e) between 2012‑2023. However, 12 % of DCs in the steel and aluminium sectors consistently under‑performed, citing technology lock‑in and high capital expenditure for retrofits. The 2023 BEE compliance audit identified 37 DCs with data‑reporting discrepancies, leading to penalty orders totalling ₹42 crore.

Sectoral Coverage Gaps and Future Directions

Despite expansion, PAT still excludes several high‑emission segments: petrochemicals (except refineries), non‑ferrous metals other than aluminium, and the burgeoning electric‑vehicle battery manufacturing cluster. The Ministry of Power’s 2024 consultation paper proposes a “PAT‑Plus” framework integrating carbon‑intensity metrics, allowing DCs to trade both ESCerts and Certified Emission Reductions (CERs) from voluntary carbon markets, and linking PAT performance to the upcoming Indian Carbon Market (ICM) under the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022. A pilot for the cement sector’s “gate‑to‑gate” methodology showed a 3.2 % additional SEC reduction compared with plant‑level baselines, informing the design of Cycle IV targets.

Mangrove Conservation and Blue Carbon Accounting: Legal Framework, Restoration Targets and International Reporting

India’s mangrove cover, estimated at 4,975 km² in the 2021 Forest Survey of India (FSI) assessment, is concentrated in the Sundarbans (West Bengal), Gujarat’s Gulf of Kachchh, Andhra Pradesh’s Godavari‑Krishna delta, and the Andaman & Nicobar Islands. The legal backbone comprises the Coastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Notification, 2019, which designates mangrove areas as CRZ‑I (ecologically sensitive) with a no‑development buffer of 50 m, and the Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980, requiring central approval for any diversion. The Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972, schedules several mangrove‑associated species (e.g., Bengal tiger, saltwater crocodile) affording additional protection.

Restoration Commitments and Institutional Arrangements

Under the Bonn Challenge, India pledged to restore 26 million hectares of degraded land by 2030, of which 1.5 million hectares are earmarked for mangrove and coastal wetland restoration. The National Mangrove Committee, chaired by the Director General of Forests, coordinates the “Mangrove Restoration Programme” (MRP) launched in 2020 with a central allocation of ₹1,200 crore for the 2020‑25 period. State‑level Mangrove Cells (established in West Bengal, Gujarat, Odisha, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu) prepare site‑specific Micro‑Plans, which must be approved by the State Coastal Zone Management Authority (SCZMA) before fund release. As of March 2024, 1,12,000 hectares have been brought under assisted natural regeneration and plantation, achieving 7.5 % of the 2030 target.

Blue Carbon Accounting and International Reporting

Blue carbon refers to carbon sequestered in coastal ecosystems — mangroves, seagrasses, salt marshes. India’s Second Biennial Update Report (BUR‑2) to the UNFCCC (submitted 2022) included a first‑time mangrove carbon stock estimate of 1.02 GtC (gigatonnes of carbon) based on the IPCC 2013 Wetlands Supplement and field plots from the Indian Council of Forestry Research and Education (ICFRE). The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) has mandated that all MRP Micro‑Plans incorporate a carbon‑stock baseline using the “Tier‑2” approach (species‑specific allometric equations, soil cores to 1 m depth). A pilot in the Sundarbans (2021‑23) demonstrated a net sequestration rate of 3.8 tC ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ after five years of restoration, feeding into the national GHG inventory for the 2026 Global Stocktake.

Challenges and Policy Levers

Key impediments include land‑tenure conflicts in the Sundarbans (forest‑dwelling communities vs. revenue land), salinity intrusion reducing survival rates, and limited long‑term financing for maintenance beyond the five‑year project cycle. The 2023 “Mangrove Conservation and Livelihoods” guideline introduces a Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) model linking blue‑carbon credits to community benefit‑sharing, aligned with the Article 6.4 mechanism. The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) is drafting a “Blue Carbon Credit” taxonomy to enable listing of mangrove‑derived credits on the Indian Carbon Market, with a pilot registry expected by 2025.

Climate Change and Human Health: National Programme on Climate Change and Human Health, Surveillance Systems and Adaptive Interventions

The National Programme on Climate Change and Human Health (NPCCHH) was launched in 2019 by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) under the National Health Mission (NHM) to address the direct and indirect health impacts of climate variability. The programme operates through a three‑tier structure: a National Climate‑Health Cell at the National Centre for Disease Control (NCDC), State Climate‑Health Cells in 28 states/UTs, and District Climate‑Health Nodal Officers. Its mandate covers heat‑related morbidity, vector‑borne disease dynamics, air‑pollution health effects, nutrition security, and mental‑health impacts of extreme events.

Surveillance and Early‑Warning Systems

NPCCHH integrates with the Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP) and the National Vector Borne Disease Control Programme (NVBDCP). A real‑time Heat‑Health Warning System (HHWS) was piloted in 2020 across 17 heat‑prone cities (including Delhi, Ahmedabad, Nagpur, Bhubaneswar) using IMD’s temperature forecasts and a threshold‑based alert matrix (yellow/orange/red). The HHWS triggers public advisories, activates cooling centres, and mobilises Accredited Social Health Activists (ASHAs) for vulnerable‑population outreach. In 2023, the system issued 1,240 alerts, with an estimated 12 % reduction in heat‑stroke admissions in participating districts compared with the 2015‑19 baseline.

Adaptive Interventions and Capacity Building

Key interventions include: (i) climate‑resilient health‑facility guidelines (retrofitting 1,200 primary health centres with passive cooling, solar backup, and water‑harvesting by 2025); (ii) a “Climate‑Smart Nutrition” module integrated into the Poshan Abhiyaan, promoting drought‑tolerant millets and bio‑fortified crops in 112 aspirational districts; (iii) training of 45,000 frontline health workers on climate‑health linkages through a blended learning platform developed with the All India Institute of Hygiene and Public Health (AIIHPH). The programme’s 2022‑23 budget was ₹350 crore, with 60 % financed through the NHM flexi‑pool and 40 % via the National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC).

Data Gaps, Governance and Future Roadmap

Despite progress, attribution of health outcomes to climate drivers remains limited — only 34 % of districts report climate‑sensitive disease data disaggregated by temperature and humidity. The NCDC’s 2023 “Climate‑Health Vulnerability Index” (CHVI) covers 640 districts but relies on proxy indicators (poverty, literacy, health‑infrastructure density) rather than epidemiological time‑series. The MoHFW’s 2024 “National Climate‑Health Action Plan” proposes: (a) mandatory climate‑health reporting in the Health Management Information System (HMIS); (b) a dedicated research fund of ₹200 crore for longitudinal cohort studies on heat‑stress and vector‑borne disease shifts; (c) institutionalising a cross‑ministerial Climate‑Health Coordination Committee (CHCC) with representation from MoEFCC, Ministry of Earth Sciences, Ministry of Jal Shakti, and the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) to ensure coherent policy across heat‑action plans, air‑quality management, and water‑security programmes.

  • Confusing the PAT scheme’s ESCerts with carbon credits under the Clean Development Mechanism; ESCerts are energy‑saving certificates, not emission‑reduction units.
  • Assuming all states have operational green‑budget tagging; only 14 states have notified guidelines and fewer have IFMIS integration.
  • Treating mangrove restoration targets under the Bonn Challenge as legally binding; they are voluntary pledges tracked through the MRP.
  • Believing the National Programme on Climate Change and Human Health covers all climate‑sensitive diseases; mental‑health and nutrition components are still pilot‑scale.
  • Overlooking that the NMSKCC’s vulnerability atlas is at district level, not sub‑district, limiting granularity for local adaptation planning.
  • Equating blue‑carbon accounting in the BUR with a fully verified carbon‑credit registry; the SEBI taxonomy for blue credits is still under development.
  • Assuming the Heat‑Health Warning System operates nationwide; it is currently active in 17 pilot cities only.

India’s Long‑Term Low‑Carbon Development Strategy 2022: Sectoral Pathways, Modelling Assumptions and Policy Levers

India submitted its Long‑Term Low‑Carbon Development Strategy (LT‑LEDS) to the UNFCCC in November 2022, fulfilling the Paris Agreement requirement for a mid‑century strategy. The document outlines two principal pathways — a “Reference” trajectory reflecting current policies and an “Enhanced Ambition” trajectory aligned with the 2070 net‑zero pledge announced at COP26. Both pathways are built on the Global Change Assessment Model (GCAM) calibrated for India, supplemented by the Integrated Resource Planning (IRP) model for the power sector and the MARKAL‑TIMES model for industry and transport.

The strategy disaggregates emissions across six sectors: electricity, industry, transport, buildings, agriculture‑forestry‑other land use (AFOLU), and waste. In the Enhanced Ambition pathway, the power sector reaches 450 GW of renewable capacity by 2030 (including 280 GW solar, 140 GW wind, 10 GW small hydro) and 900 GW by 2050, driven by a phased coal‑plant retirement schedule that caps new coal additions at 5 GW per year after 2025. Green hydrogen production targets 5 million tonnes per annum by 2030, supported by the National Green Hydrogen Mission’s ₹19,744 crore outlay and a dedicated electrolyser manufacturing incentive of ₹4,500 crore.

Industrial decarbonisation relies on the Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) scheme’s Cycle‑VII targets, mandatory energy‑audit compliance for designated consumers, and a CCUS pilot pipeline at Koyambedu (Tamil Nadu) and Tuticorin (Tamil Nadu) with a combined capture capacity of 1.2 MtCO₂/yr by 2027. The transport pathway assumes 30 % electric‑vehicle (EV) sales share for two‑wheelers and 15 % for passenger cars by 2030, underpinned by the FAME‑II subsidy extension (₹10,000 crore) and a national charging‑infrastructure target of 2.5 million public chargers by 2030.

Buildings sector modelling adopts the Energy Conservation Building Code (ECBC) 2022 as mandatory for new commercial floor area >1,000 sq m in 22 states, with a retrofit programme targeting 1.5 million sq m of existing stock by 2025, financed through the National Housing Bank’s Green Building Refinance Facility (₹2,000 crore). AFOLU mitigation hinges on the National Mission for a Green India’s 5 million hectare afforestation target and the Bonn Challenge pledge of 26 million hectares restored by 2030, translated into a carbon sink of 2.5 GtCO₂e/yr in the Enhanced pathway.

Policy levers identified include: (i) a carbon‑price corridor via the proposed Indian Carbon Market (ICM) with a floor price of ₹800/tCO₂e by 2026; (ii) fiscal incentives for electrolyser manufacturing and battery‑cell production under the Production‑Linked Incentive (PLI) schemes; (iii) a dedicated Climate Finance Facility under the National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC) to de‑risk private investment in CCUS and green hydrogen; and (iv) a regulatory mandate for annual disclosure of scope‑1,‑2 and‑3 emissions for all designated consumers under the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act 2022.

National Mission on Sustainable Habitat: Building Energy Codes, Retrofit Programmes and Financing Mechanisms

The National Mission on Sustainable Habitat (NMSH) was launched in June 2010 as one of the eight core missions under the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC). Its mandate covers urban planning, waste‑to‑energy, sustainable transport, and, critically, energy efficiency in the building sector. The mission’s flagship regulatory instrument is the Energy Conservation Building Code (ECBC), first notified in 2007, substantially revised in 2017 and again in 2022 to align with the Bureau of Energy Efficiency’s (BEE) star‑rating framework for commercial buildings.

The ECBC 2022 sets a maximum Energy Performance Index (EPI) of 110 kWh/m²/yr for new commercial buildings in composite climate zones, 90 kWh/m²/yr for warm‑humid zones, and 70 kWh/m²/yr for cold zones. Compliance is mandatory for all new government buildings and for private commercial constructions exceeding 1,000 sq m in 22 states that have notified the code under their respective building bye‑laws. As of March 2024, 18 states have integrated ECBC 2022 into their municipal building approval workflow, covering an estimated 35 % of the national commercial floor‑area pipeline.

Retrofit action is driven by the “Deep Retrofit Programme” (DRP) launched in 2021 under the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA). The DRP targets 1.5 million sq m of existing government‑owned building stock by 2025, focusing on envelope insulation, high‑efficiency HVAC, LED lighting, and building‑management‑system (BMS) integration. By December 2023, 0.92 million sq m had been retrofitted, delivering an average 28 % reduction in annual electricity consumption per retrofitted building, verified through BEE’s “Building Energy Performance Certification” (BEPC) audits.

Financing mechanisms combine public‑budget allocations with market‑based instruments. The National Housing Bank (NHB) operates a Green Building Refinance Facility (GBRF) with a corpus of ₹2,000 crore, offering concessional refinance at 0.5 % below the prevailing repo rate for loans that meet ECBC‑compliant or BEPC‑certified standards. The Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) administers a “Green Building Credit Line” of ₹1,200 crore for MSME‑owned commercial properties, with a 1 % interest subvention for projects achieving a minimum 3‑star BEE rating. Additionally, the Ministry of Finance’s 2023 “Green Budget Tagging” exercise earmarked ₹3,500 crore for NMSH‑linked projects across central ministries, with 60 % routed through the NHB‑GBRF and 40 % through state‑level Urban Development Funds.

Monitoring is anchored in the “Urban Energy Dashboard” hosted on the Smart Cities Mission portal, which aggregates real‑time EPI data from smart‑metered buildings in 100 mission cities. The dashboard feeds into the NMSH’s annual progress report submitted to the Prime Minister’s Council on Climate Change, enabling mid‑course correction of retrofit targets and code‑adoption incentives.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about the Energy Conservation Building Code (ECBC) 2022 is correct? (a) It is mandatory for all residential buildings above 500 sq m. (b) It sets a uniform EPI of 100 kWh/m²/yr across all climate zones. (c) It applies to new commercial buildings >1,000 sq m in states that have notified the code. (d) It is enforced by the Central Pollution Control Board.”
Correct answer: (c). The ECBC 2022 is a model code; it becomes mandatory only when a state notifies it under its building bye‑laws. It applies to new commercial buildings exceeding 1,000 sq m, not residential. The EPI limits vary by climate zone (110, 90, 70 kWh/m²/yr). Enforcement rests with municipal building‑approval authorities, not the CPCB.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question references a mission under NAPCC, first identify the mission’s core sector (habitat, water, solar, etc.) and then recall the specific regulatory tool (ECBC for habitat, PAT for industry, FAME for transport). Match the tool to the correct ministry and the latest amendment year — examiners frequently test the 2022 ECBC revision versus the 2017 version.

Climate‑Related Financial Disclosures for Non‑Listed Entities: RBI’s Draft Guidelines and Sectoral Impact

In March 2024 the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) released the “Draft Framework for Climate‑Related Financial Disclosures for Non‑Listed Entities” (CL‑FDI), extending the Task Force on Climate‑Related Financial Disclosures (TCFD) alignment beyond scheduled commercial banks and listed corporates. The draft targets Non‑Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs) with asset size ≥ ₹500 crore, Housing Finance Companies (HFCs) above ₹1,000 crore, and unlisted corporates with annual turnover ≥ ₹1,000 crore or outstanding bank credit ≥ ₹500 crore. The framework proposes a phased implementation: Phase 1 (FY 2025‑26) — governance and strategy disclosures; Phase 2 (FY 2026‑27) — risk‑management and metrics; Phase 3 (FY 2027‑28) — scenario analysis and transition‑plan reporting.

Governance requirements mandate board‑level oversight of climate risk, a dedicated climate‑risk committee, and integration of climate metrics into executive compensation. Strategy disclosures must articulate short‑ (0‑3 yr), medium‑ (3‑10 yr) and long‑term (10‑30 yr) climate‑related risks and opportunities, referencing the entity’s exposure to high‑carbon sectors (thermal power, cement, steel, aviation) and physical‑risk hotspots (coastal assets, flood‑prone agricultural land). Risk‑management section requires a climate‑risk heat map, stress‑testing methodology (minimum two scenarios: IEA‑NZ2050 and NGFS‑Current‑Policies), and a description of mitigation actions such as portfolio decarbonisation targets or green‑asset tagging.

Metrics and targets include: (i) financed emissions (Scope 3 Category 15) reported in tCO₂e using the Partnership for Carbon Accounting Financials (PCAF) methodology; (ii) green‑asset ratio (green‑labelled loans / total loan book) with a minimum 5 % target by FY 2027‑28 for NBFCs; (iii) physical‑risk exposure index for collateral‑backed lending, derived from the National Remote Sensing Centre’s flood‑and‑cyclone hazard layers. Scenario analysis must be quantitative, employing the Network for Greening the Financial System (NGFS) scenarios, and results disclosed in the annual financial statements’ “Notes to Accounts”.

Sectoral impact assessments conducted by the Indian Banks’ Association (IBA) indicate that real‑estate lending (≈ ₹12 lakh crore) faces the highest transition risk due to ECBC‑driven retrofit costs, while agriculture credit (≈ ₹18 lakh crore) is most vulnerable to physical risk from erratic monsoons. MSME portfolios (≈ ₹25 lakh crore) exhibit limited data availability for Scope 3 estimation, prompting the RBI to allow a “proxy‑emission factor” approach for the first two reporting cycles. The draft also proposes a “Climate Disclosure Repository” hosted on the RBI’s Centralised Information Management System (CIMS) for public access, with a compliance‑monitoring cell to issue supervisory letters for non‑compliance after Phase 2.

India’s Participation in the Global Stocktake 2023: Negotiating Positions, Data Submission and Domestic Follow‑Up

The first Global Stocktake (GST) under the Paris Agreement concluded at COP28 in Dubai (November‑December 2023). India’s engagement spanned three tracks: the Technical Dialogue (TD), the Political Phase, and the subsequent domestic implementation agenda. In the TD (June‑September 2023), India submitted its Third Biennial Update Report (BUR‑3) covering GHG inventory for 2019 (2.88 GtCO₂e, 7 % increase over 2016), mitigation actions across the eight NAPCC missions, and adaptation progress under the National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC). The BUR‑3 was prepared using the 2006 IPCC Guidelines and the UNFCCC Common Reporting Format (CRF) tables, with quality‑assurance by the National Institute of Public Finance and Policy (NIPFP).

India’s negotiating positions emphasised: (i) equity and Common But Differentiated Responsibilities and Respective Capabilities (CBDR‑RC), arguing that the GST’s “collective progress” metric must weight historical emissions and per‑capita income; (ii) the need for scaled‑up climate finance, reiterating the $100 billion per year goal and demanding a clear roadmap for the new collective quantified goal (NCQG) post‑2025; (iii) technology transfer under Article 10, seeking removal of intellectual‑property barriers for critical mitigation technologies (electrolysers, CCUS, advanced batteries); and (iv) loss‑and‑damage finance, supporting the establishment of the Loss and Damage Fund with a governance structure that ensures direct access for vulnerable developing countries.

Data submission for the GST included: (a) the updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) 2022, raising the non‑fossil‑fuel electricity capacity target to 500 GW by 2030 and committing to 45 % reduction in emissions intensity of GDP from 2005 levels; (b) the LT‑LEDS 2022 (discussed above) as the long‑term strategy; (c) the National Communication (NATCOM‑3) 2023, providing a comprehensive vulnerability assessment for 640 districts using the Climate Vulnerability Index (CVI). The GST’s “Enhanced Transparency Framework” (ETF) required India to adopt the Modalities, Procedures and Guidelines (MPGs) for reporting, prompting the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) to launch the “ETF‑India Portal” in January 2024 for automated CRF‑table generation.

Domestic follow‑up actions announced in the 2024 Union Budget include: (i) a ₹1,000 crore “GST Implementation Fund” to strengthen state‑level GHG inventory capacities; (ii) mandatory integration of GST‑derived mitigation gaps into the State Action Plans on Climate Change (SAPCCs) by March 2025; (iii) a new “Carbon Budgeting” pilot for the power sector, allocating annual emission allowances to designated consumers aligned with the 2030 NDC trajectory; and (iv) an inter‑ministerial “GST Coordination Committee” chaired by the Cabinet Secretary to monitor progress on finance, technology and capacity‑building commitments made at COP28.

  • Confusing the LT‑LEDS “Enhanced Ambition” pathway with the NDC targets; the LT‑LEDS extends to 2050/2070 while the NDC covers 2030 only.
  • Assuming ECBC 2022 is uniformly mandatory nationwide; it becomes binding only after state notification.
  • Treating the RBI’s draft CL‑FDI as already enforceable for all NBFCs; phased implementation begins FY 2025‑26 and applies only above the asset‑size thresholds.
  • Believing the Global Stocktake’s outcome directly imposes new legal obligations on India; the GST is a collective assessment, not a treaty amendment.
  • Overlooking that the BUR‑3 uses 2006 IPCC Guidelines, not the 2019 Refinement, affecting comparability with later inventories.
  • Equating the “green‑asset ratio” target for NBFCs with a regulatory capital requirement; it is a disclosure target, not a capital adequacy norm.
  • Assuming the Loss and Damage Fund established at COP27 is operational for Indian states; the fund’s governance and disbursement modalities are still under negotiation.

These advanced sections deepen the aspirant’s grasp of India’s evolving climate architecture, linking policy design, financial regulation and international negotiation dynamics.

Compensatory Afforestation Fund Management and Planning Authority: Fund Flows, State CAMPA Performance and Supreme Court Monitoring

The Compensatory Afforestation Fund Act, 2016 (CAF Act) operationalised the Supreme Court’s 2002 direction in T.N. Godavarman Thirumulpad v. Union of India to institutionalise the collection and utilisation of compensatory afforestation (CA) levies. The Act established the National Compensatory Afforestation Fund (NCAF) under the Public Account of India and State Compensatory Afforestation Funds (SCAFs) under respective state public accounts. Funds accrue from three primary heads: (i) compensatory afforestation charges (net present value of forest land diverted), (ii) additional compensatory afforestation charges, and (iii) penal compensatory afforestation for violations. As of March 2024, the cumulative corpus across national and state funds exceeded ₹75,000 crore, with annual inflows averaging ₹6,000–7,000 crore.

The National Authority, chaired by the Union Minister for Environment, Forest and Climate Change, approves the Annual Plan of Operations (APOs) submitted by State CAMPAs. The 2018 CAF Rules mandate that 80 per cent of the fund be used for afforestation, regeneration, and wildlife protection, while 20 per cent covers infrastructure, capacity building, and monitoring. However, Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) reports (2019, 2022) and the Supreme Court’s monitoring committee (constituted in 2019) have flagged persistent gaps: (a) low utilisation rates — several states spent less than 40 per cent of available balances; (b) diversion of funds for non-forestry activities such as office buildings and vehicle purchases; (c) weak survival monitoring — geo-tagged plantation data is absent for over 60 per cent of reported hectares in states like Odisha, Jharkhand, and Madhya Pradesh; and (d) delayed APO approvals causing fund lock-in.

The Supreme Court’s 2023 order in In re: T.N. Godavarman Thirumulpad directed the National Authority to: (i) implement a real-time, GIS-based plantation monitoring dashboard accessible to the public; (ii) mandate third-party survival audits at years 1, 3, and 5; (iii) recover misutilised amounts with interest from state forest departments; and (iv) link future fund releases to verified survival rates exceeding 70 per cent. The Ministry’s 2024 guidelines now require State CAMPAs to upload polygon-level plantation boundaries on the e-Green Watch portal before fund release. A critical unresolved issue is the treatment of interest accrued on unspent balances — states argue it belongs to them, while the Centre contends it must be ploughed back into the corpus. The Court has referred this to the National Authority for a binding formula.

Production-Linked Incentive Scheme for High-Efficiency Solar PV Modules: Tranche-wise Allocation, Domestic Value Addition Requirements and Supply Chain Outcomes

The Production-Linked Incentive (PLI) Scheme for High-Efficiency Solar PV Modules, notified in April 2021 with an outlay of ₹4,500 crore (Tranche I) and expanded by ₹19,500 crore in the 2022–23 Budget (Tranche II), targets the creation of 65 GW of fully integrated manufacturing capacity across polysilicon, ingots, wafers, cells, and modules. Tranche I (₹4,500 crore) allocated capacity to three selected bidders — Reliance New Energy Solar (20 GW), Adani Solar (4 GW), and First Solar India (3.4 GW) — through a transparent bidding process based on the lowest incentive per MW quoted. Tranche II (₹19,500 crore) opened bidding in 2023 with a revised design: (i) mandatory domestic value addition (DVA) of 40 per cent for modules and 25 per cent for cells in Year 1, escalating to 60 per cent and 40 per cent respectively by Year 5; (ii) incentive payable only on modules sold, not manufactured; (iii) a cap of ₹300 crore per GW per annum for integrated plants and ₹150 crore per GW for module-only units; and (iv) a minimum capacity commitment of 1 GW for integrated and 2 GW for module-only bids.

The scheme’s DVA calculation follows the Phased Manufacturing Programme methodology: DVA = (Ex-factory price – Value of imported inputs) ÷ Ex-factory price × 100. Imported inputs include polysilicon, wafers, encapsulants, backsheets, and glass unless domestically sourced. The 2023 bid results awarded 39.6 GW across 11 companies, including Tata Power Solar (4.3 GW), Waaree Energies (6 GW), and Premier Energies (4 GW). Notably, no polysilicon or ingot/wafer capacity was bid in Tranche I; Tranche II saw commitments for 10 GW of polysilicon (by Reliance and Adani) and 15 GW of ingot/wafer capacity, addressing the deepest import dependency — China supplies over 95 per cent of India’s polysilicon and wafer imports.

Early outcomes (as of March 2024): 8.7 GW of module capacity and 2.1 GW of cell capacity commissioned under Tranche I; Tranche II awardees have until March 2026 (modules) and March 2027 (integrated) for commissioning. The scheme’s interaction with the Approved List of Models and Manufacturers (ALMM) — reinstated in March 2024 after a two-year suspension — creates a de facto domestic procurement preference for government-supported projects. However, two structural risks persist: (a) the DVA threshold does not distinguish between low-value assembly (framing, junction-boxing) and high-value cell processing, potentially incentivising shallow integration; and (b) the incentive is a post-sale reimbursement, exposing manufacturers to working-capital gaps and policy discontinuity risk. The Ministry of New and Renewable Energy’s 2024 consultation paper proposes a front-loaded capital subsidy for polysilicon and ingot/wafer units to de-risk the highest-capex segments.

Examiner's Trap: Conflating PLI Incentive with Capital Subsidy
A question states: "The PLI Scheme for Solar PV Modules provides a capital subsidy of up to 40 per cent of project cost for integrated manufacturing units." Identify the error and explain the correct incentive structure.
The statement is incorrect. The PLI scheme provides a production-linked incentive — a per-unit (₹/W) payment on modules sold over five years — not an upfront capital subsidy. The incentive is capped at ₹300 crore per GW per annum for integrated plants and is reimbursed post-sale upon verification of domestic value addition and sales invoices. No capital subsidy component exists in the notified scheme guidelines. The confusion often arises from the separate Modified Special Incentive Package Scheme (M-SIPS) or state-level capital subsidies, which are distinct instruments.
Exam-Day Strategy
When a question asks about "domestic value addition" under any PLI scheme, always specify the formula (Ex-factory price minus imported inputs divided by ex-factory price) and the escalation timeline (Year 1 vs Year 5 thresholds). For solar PLI, quote the 40%/60% module DVA and 25%/40% cell DVA trajectory. This precision distinguishes a 12-marker answer from a generic 6-marker.

Airshed-Based Air Quality Management: Graded Response Action Plan Stages, Emergency Measures and Interstate Coordination Mechanism under CAQM

The airshed approach recognises that air pollution transcends administrative boundaries, requiring coordinated action across a shared meteorological and topographical zone. The Indo-Gangetic Plain (IGP) — spanning Punjab, Haryana, Uttar Pradesh, Rajasthan, Delhi, and Bihar — constitutes India’s most critical airshed, contributing 40–50 per cent of the national PM2.5 burden. The Commission for Air Quality Management in National Capital Region and Adjoining Areas (CAQM), established under the 2021 Act, is the first statutory body with explicit airshed-level jurisdiction. Its 2022 Comprehensive Policy for Air Pollution Control in NCR formalised the Graded Response Action Plan (GRAP) into four stages linked to the Air Quality Index (AQI): Stage I (Poor, AQI 201–300), Stage II (Very Poor, 301–400), Stage III (Severe, 401–450), and Stage IV (Severe+, >450).

Each stage triggers escalating measures: Stage I activates mechanical sweeping, dust suppression at construction sites, and strict enforcement of pollution-under-control (PUC) certificates. Stage II adds a ban on coal/firewood in hotels/restaurants, enhanced parking fees, and targeted action on identified hotspots. Stage III (Severe) mandates: (i) closure of stone crushers and mining in NCR; (ii) ban on construction/demolition (except linear public projects); (iii) daily water sprinkling on roads; (iv) restriction on BS-III petrol and BS-IV diesel four-wheelers in Delhi; and (v) state governments to decide on school closures. Stage IV (Severe+) adds: (i) entry ban on trucks (except essentials/CNG/electric); (ii) ban on four-wheelers (except BS-VI, CNG, electric) in Delhi; (iii) work-from-home for 50 per cent government/private staff; and (iv) potential emergency meeting of the CAQM to direct interstate measures.

The CAQM’s interstate coordination mechanism operates through: (a) the Sub-Committee on GRAP (chaired by CAQM Member-Secretary, with member-secretaries of state pollution control boards) meeting daily during Stages III–IV; (b) the Inter-State Coordination Committee (chief secretaries of NCR states) for policy-level decisions such as synchronised stubble-burning mitigation, brick-kiln zig-zag conversion deadlines, and industrial fuel-switching timelines; and (c) the Air Quality Forecasting Cell (IITM Pune, IMD, CPCB) providing 72-hour AQI forecasts to pre-emptively trigger stages. The 2023–24 winter saw GRAP Stage III invoked for 22 days and Stage IV for 6 days in Delhi-NCR. Post-season analysis by CPCB indicated that Stage IV measures reduced peak PM2.5 by 15–20 per cent relative to counterfactual modelling, but compliance gaps persisted: only 60 per cent of construction sites implemented dust controls, and BS-III/IV vehicle restrictions were enforced at 45 per cent of checkpoints. The CAQM’s 2024 directive mandates real-time emission monitoring (CEMS) for all red-category industries in NCR and integration with the PRANA portal for automated GRAP triggering.

District Climate Resilience Plans: Vulnerability Assessment Integration, Convergence with MGNREGS and Fifteenth Finance Commission Grants

The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change’s 2023 Guidelines for Preparation of District Climate Resilience Plans (DCRPs) operationalise the National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC) mandate to mainstream adaptation at the sub-state level. Each DCRP must integrate: (i) the Climate Vulnerability Index (CVI) for the district — computed from 38 indicators across exposure, sensitivity, and adaptive capacity dimensions as per the 2021 Climate Vulnerability Assessment for Adaptation Planning in India (IIT Mandi, IISc Bangalore, supported by DST); (ii) sectoral risk profiles for agriculture, water, health, forests, and infrastructure; and (iii) a prioritised list of adaptation interventions with cost estimates, implementing agencies, and timelines. As of March 2024, 312 districts across 27 states have submitted draft DCRPs; 189 have been approved by State Steering Committees.

The critical innovation is the mandated convergence architecture: (a) MGNREGS — at least 60 per cent of DCRP adaptation works (water harvesting, afforestation, land development, flood protection) must be executed through MGNREGS labour budget, with the District Programme Coordinator as nodal officer; (b) Fifteenth Finance Commission (XV-FC) tied grants — the 2021–26 XV-FC allocated ₹1.42 lakh crore for local bodies, with 60 per cent earmarked for water/sanitation and 40 per cent untied; DCRPs must map adaptation works to these grant windows; (c) State Disaster Response Fund (SDRF) / National Disaster Response Fund (NDRF) — pre-disaster mitigation measures (up to 10 per cent of SDRF) can fund DCRP-identified structural measures; and (d) NAFCC project pipeline — district-level proposals feed state NAFCC submissions to the National Steering Committee.

Implementation gaps documented in the 2023–24 MoEFCC review: (i) CVI integration is superficial — 70 per cent of DCRPs use state-level vulnerability rankings without district-specific ground-truthing; (ii) MGNREGS convergence fails where technical sanction capacities are weak — only 35 per cent of planned water-harvesting structures received timely technical estimates; (iii) XV-FC grant utilisation for adaptation is untracked — no separate budget code exists for "climate resilience" in panchayat accounts; (iv) DCRPs lack private-sector and community organisation partnerships — less than 5 per cent mention FPOs, SHGs, or CSR linkages. The Ministry’s 2024 advisory requires: (a) GIS-based asset mapping of all MGNREGS/XV-FC works tagged to DCRP interventions; (b) quarterly convergence review by the District Collector with line departments; and (c) a "Climate Resilience Budget Statement" in the district annual budget from FY 2025–26.

Vehicle Scrapping Policy and Registered Vehicle Scrapping Facilities: Incentive Structure, Steel Scrap Quality Standards and Circular Economy Linkages

The Vehicle Scrapping Policy, launched in August 2021 and operationalised through the Motor Vehicles (Registration and Functions of Vehicle Scrapping Facility) Rules, 2021, targets the phased removal of unfit and polluting vehicles. The policy mandates fitness testing for commercial vehicles older than 15 years and private vehicles older than 20 years at Automated Testing Stations (ATS). Vehicles failing the test are designated "End-of-Life Vehicles" (ELVs) and must be scrapped at Registered Vehicle Scrapping Facilities (RVSFs). As of March 2024, 62 RVSFs have been approved across 18 states, with a combined annual capacity of 1.2 million vehicles.

The incentive structure combines disincentives and incentives: (i) Disincentives — increased re-registration fees (10–25 times for commercial, 8–15 times for private), mandatory fitness certification fees, green tax (up to 10–25 per cent of road tax) for older vehicles in non-attainment cities; (ii) Incentives — scrap value (4–6 per cent of ex-showroom price), road-tax rebate (up to 25 per cent for personal, 15 per cent for commercial), manufacturer discount (5 per cent on new vehicle purchase against scrapping certificate), and registration-fee waiver. The scrapping certificate (Certificate of Deposit) is tradable — the vehicle owner can sell it to a prospective buyer who claims the manufacturer discount.

Steel scrap quality is governed by the Steel Scrap Recycling Policy, 2019 and the Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) IS 13932:2023 for "Ferrous Scrap — Specification". RVSFs must produce shredded scrap meeting Grade 1 (clean, dense, homogeneous) or Grade 2 (light melting scrap) standards, with maximum 0.3 per cent copper, 0.15 per cent tin, and 0.05 per cent lead by weight. The 2023 Steel Ministry directive requires domestic steel mills to source a minimum of 25 per cent of scrap input from authorised RVSFs by 2025–26, rising to 50 per cent by 2030. This creates a demand pull for high-quality scrap, addressing the current 30 per cent import dependence (6–7 million tonnes annually).

Circular economy linkages extend beyond steel: (a) Aluminium — RVSFs must segregate aluminium components (engine blocks, wheels, radiators) for secondary smelters; (b) Plastics and rubber — tyres go to pyrolysis or crumb-rubber units; plastic bumpers/dashboards to recyclers; (c) Rare earths — magnets from motors, sensors, and catalytic converters (platinum, palladium, rhodium) are recovered under the E-Waste (Management) Rules, 2022, which now cover automotive electronics. The policy’s 2024 amendment mandates RVSFs to upload material-wise recovery data on the VAHAN portal, enabling mass-balance tracking. A key challenge: the informal sector still handles 70–80 per cent of ELVs, bypassing depollution (fluid drainage, refrigerant recovery) and yielding low-grade contaminated scrap. The 2024 CAQM directive for NCR prohibits non-RVSF scrapping and imposes environmental compensation of ₹50,000 per vehicle on violators.

  • Confusing the Compensatory Afforestation Fund (CAF) corpus with annual budgetary allocation — the CAF is a non-lapsable fund under the Public Account, not a budget head.
  • Assuming the PLI incentive for solar PV is a capital subsidy; it is a production-linked, post-sale reimbursement subject to domestic value addition verification.
  • Treating GRAP stages as discretionary advisories; Stages III and IV are legally binding directions under the CAQM Act, 2021, with penal provisions for non-compliance.
  • Believing District Climate Resilience Plans are standalone documents; they are convergence instruments that must map interventions to MGNREGS, XV-FC grants, and SDRF/NDRF windows.
  • Equating the Vehicle Scrapping Policy’s "scrap value" with the total incentive; the scrap value (4–6% of ex-showroom price) is only one component alongside road-tax rebate, manufacturer discount, and registration-fee waiver.
  • Overlooking that the CAF Act’s 80:20 utilisation rule applies to the state fund, while the national fund follows a 90:10 split for afforestation versus infrastructure.
  • Assuming all RVSFs automatically produce BIS Grade 1 scrap; certification requires third-party testing, and many facilities currently output Grade 2 or ungraded shredded material.

Environmental Liability Regime: Public Liability Insurance Act 1991, Environmental Damage Compensation and the Proposed Environmental Liability Bill

The Public Liability Insurance Act (PLIA) 1991 remains the statutory backbone for compulsory insurance of hazardous industries. It obliges every owner of a hazardous unit to take out a policy covering third‑party bodily injury, property damage and environmental degradation up to ₹5 crore per incident and ₹15 crore in aggregate per policy year. The 2022 amendment (Notification S.O. 1234(E), 15 March 2022) raised the per‑incident limit to ₹10 crore and introduced a mandatory “environmental damage” cover of ₹2 crore, aligning the Act with the polluter‑pays principle articulated in the National Green Tribunal (NGT) judgments of 2017 (Vellore Citizens Welfare Forum) and 2020 (M.C. Mehta v. Union of India).

Complementing PLIA, the NGT has evolved an Environmental Damage Compensation (EDC) framework that quantifies ecological loss through the “restoration cost” method. In the 2021 order on the Sterlite copper smelter (Tamil Nadu), the Tribunal directed a ₹100 crore compensation package based on a scientific assessment of soil, water and air contamination, setting a precedent for monetary valuation of ecosystem services. The EDC orders are enforceable as decrees under the NGT Act, 2010, and non‑compliance attracts contempt proceedings.

The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) circulated the draft Environmental Liability Bill, 2022 for public consultation. The Bill proposes a strict‑liability regime for “significant environmental damage” defined as adverse change exceeding thresholds in the Environment (Protection) Rules, 1986. Key features include: (a) a Central Environmental Liability Fund (CELF) capitalised by a 0.5 per cent cess on turnover of hazardous sectors; (b) joint and several liability for operators, directors and lenders; (c) a statutory limitation period of 30 years from the date of damage discovery; and (d) provision for “restoration bonds” that must be posted before project commissioning. The Bill also empowers the Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) to issue “damage assessment notices” and to recover costs directly from the CELF.

Implementation gaps persist. As of March 2024, only 62 per cent of the 12,000‑odd hazardous units registered under PLIA have valid policies, and the CELF corpus stands at ₹1,200 crore against an estimated liability exposure of ₹1.8 lakh crore. The NGT’s 2023 directive to the Ministry to operationalise the CELF within six months remains pending. For aspirants, the interplay between PLIA’s insurance‑based compensation, NGT’s restoration‑cost jurisprudence, and the proposed strict‑liability Bill constitutes a three‑layer liability architecture that examiners frequently test through scenario‑based questions.

Renewable Energy Curtailment Management: Grid Code Revisions, Forecasting Obligations and Compensation Framework

Curtailment of wind and solar generation has risen from 2.3 per cent of total renewable output in 2019‑20 to 5.8 per cent in 2023‑24, driven by transmission bottlenecks in the Green Energy Corridors and inadequate forecasting. The Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC) responded with the Grid Code (Amendment) Regulations, 2023 (Notification No. L‑1/2023‑CERC, 30 June 2023), which introduced three binding mechanisms.

First, all renewable generators above 5 MW must submit day‑ahead and intra‑day generation forecasts to the respective Regional Load Despatch Centres (RLDCs) with a maximum absolute error of 15 per cent for wind and 10 per cent for solar. Non‑compliance attracts a penalty of ₹0.05/kWh for each 1 per cent deviation beyond the threshold, capped at 5 per cent of the monthly energy charge.

Second, the “Curtailment Compensation Mechanism” (CCM) mandates that distribution licensees pay generators 50 per cent of the applicable tariff for the first two hours of curtailment in a settlement period, 75 per cent for the next two hours, and 100 per cent thereafter. The compensation is settled through the Deviation Settlement Mechanism (DSM) pool, and the RLDCs publish weekly curtailment reports on the POSOCO portal.

Third, the amendment requires transmission licensees to declare “Available Transfer Capability” (ATC) for each corridor on a rolling 15‑minute basis. If ATC falls below the scheduled renewable injection, the RLDC must issue a “curtailment instruction” with a reason code (e.g., “thermal limit”, “voltage stability”). The generator may challenge the instruction before the Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (APTEL) within 30 days.

Operational data from the first nine months of 2024 show that the CCM reduced average curtailment duration from 4.2 hours to 2.7 hours per event in the Southern Region, while forecasting compliance improved to 78 per cent of generators meeting the error band. However, the compensation payout has increased the DSM pool liability by ₹1,100 crore, prompting the Ministry of Power to propose a “Curtailment Reserve Fund” financed by a 0.2 per cent surcharge on renewable tariffs. The interplay of forecasting penalties, tiered compensation, and ATC transparency forms a core technical‑regulatory nexus that appears in both GS‑III economy and technology questions.

Technique
A 200 MW solar park in Rajasthan is curtailed for three consecutive hours due to a transmission line thermal limit. The applicable tariff is ₹2.50/kWh. Calculate the compensation payable under the CERC Curtailment Compensation Mechanism for the three‑hour period.
Under the CCM, compensation is tiered: first two hours at 50 per cent of tariff, next two hours at 75 per cent. For a three‑hour curtailment, the first two hours receive 50 per cent × ₹2.50 = ₹1.25/kWh; the third hour receives 75 per cent × ₹2.50 = ₹1.875/kWh. Energy curtailed per hour = 200 MW × 1 h = 200 MWh = 200,000 kWh. Compensation = (2 h × 200,000 kWh × ₹1.25) + (1 h × 200,000 kWh × ₹1.875) = ₹500,00,000 + ₹375,00,000 = ₹8.75 crore.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the three‑tier compensation percentages (50 / 75 / 100) and the forecasting error bands (15 % wind, 10 % solar). In numerical questions, convert MW to MWh first, then apply the tiered rates. For descriptive answers, link the CCM to the DSM pool and the proposed Curtailment Reserve Fund to show systemic understanding.

Sustainable Cooling and HFC Phase‑Down: National Cooling Action Plan, Kigali Amendment Implementation and Energy Efficiency Labelling

India’s National Cooling Action Plan (NCAP), launched in March 2019, sets a 20‑year roadmap to reduce cooling demand by 20‑25 per cent and refrigerant demand by 25‑30 per cent by 2037‑38, relative to a 2017‑18 baseline. The plan aligns with the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, which India ratified in September 2021. The Amendment mandates a stepwise HFC phase‑down: freeze consumption at 2024 levels, 10 per cent reduction by 2029, 30 per cent by 2035, 50 per cent by 2040, and 80 per cent by 2045 (baseline: average 2020‑22 consumption). The Ozone Cell, MoEFCC, notified the HFC Phase‑Down Schedule (Notification S.O. 4567(E), 12 January 2023) translating these targets into annual allocation quotas for manufacturers and importers.

On the appliance side, the Bureau of Energy Efficiency (BEE) revised the Star Labelling Programme for Room Air Conditioners (RACs) effective 1 January 2023. The minimum Indian Seasonal Energy Efficiency Ratio (ISEER) for a 1‑star split AC was raised from 3.10 to 3.30, and a new 5‑star threshold of ISEER ≥ 5.0 was introduced. The programme now covers variable‑speed inverter ACs, which constitute 68 per cent of the 2023 market.
